Tides of History Directory

A Screen-Reader-Friendly Guide to Patrick Wyman’s Tides of History Podcast

Jamal Mazrui

September 2026

Abstract

A screen-reader-friendly directory of the podcast Tides of History, covering 390 public episodes from 2017 to 2026. Each episode links to its audio and lists its metadata in separate, alphabetically sorted fields.

Tides of History Directory

Tides of History, hosted by historian and writer Patrick Wyman for Wondery, explores the deep forces and turning points that shaped the modern world, blending narrative storytelling with interviews and scholarship.

This directory covers 390 public episodes, 2017 to 2026. Each episode’s title link plays its audio; an Episode page link follows the title when the show offers a separate page; and the metadata on record appears one kind per field, in alphabetical order: Date, Duration, Episode, Season, and Summary, each shown only when available. Titles ignore a leading “A”, “An”, or “The”; people are sorted by surname; all sorting ignores case.

Episodes are grouped by year, earliest first, and within each group they are in date order, oldest first, so a show reads the way it unfolded. Appendixes reorder them by date (newest first), by title.

For current details, the linked pages are authoritative. This directory was compiled by Jamal Mazrui from the show’s public feed.

Table of Contents

  • Appendix B: Episodes by Title

The Bell Beaker Phenomenon and the Rise of the Bronze Age

Date: June 10, 2021

Duration: 00:47:51

Episode: 44

Season: 4

Summary: Around 4,500 years ago, bell-shaped ceramic drinking vessels called “beakers” begin showing up with the dead in tombs all over western Europe. Everywhere from Portugal to Sicily to Scotland to Slovakia, these distinctive containers show up, often accompanied by archery equipment and upheaval in the societies established in these places. The Bronze Age and metallurgy would soon follow. But what were the Beakers for? Who used them, and why? Thanks to ancient DNA and cutting-edge archaeological science, we can explore the Beaker Phenomenon in all its manifestations, from marriage networks and trade to prehistoric genocide. What was the Indus Valley Civilization? Interview with Dr. Adam Green

Date: April 7, 2022

Duration: 00:49:48

Episode: 88

Season: 4

Summary: The Indus Valley Civilization doesn’t get much attention compared to Mesopotamia or Egypt, but it covered an area of a million square kilometers, was home to hundreds of thousands or millions of people and a unified culture, and lasted for the better part of a millennium. More than that, the Indus Civilization doesn’t seem to fit the models we have for how early states functioned. Dr. Adam Green of Cambridge University joins me to explain the unusual way in which the Indus Civilization was organized, its lack of powerful elites, and how and why it eventually fell apart. Date: May 12, 2022

Duration: 00:42:56

Summary: Mesoamerica is one of only a few places in the world where “civilization” - states, writing, cities, monumental building, and so on - emerged independently. Today the Olmecs are known mostly for their colossal carved stone heads, but they were the pioneers of a distinctively Mesoamerican form of civilization that lasted for millennia. Ancient Slaveries

Date: January 29, 2026

Duration: 00:35:42

Episode: 165

Season: 5

Summary: Slavery was foundational to ancient societies, but it was never a single thing: The experiences of the enslaved varied dramatically depending on when and where they lived, who owned them, and most of all, the jobs they had to do. Slavery was never good, but there were better and worse versions, and in this episode, we’ll explore some of the variation that shaped the lives of enslaved people.
